REPTILES ▶ AMAZON TREE BOA A nocturnal hunter, the Amazon tree boa catches birds, lizards, frogs, and small mammals in the treetops, and kills its prey with constriction. The snake coils around its prey and gradually increases the pressure until the victim’s heart stops beating. The lifeless animal is then swallowed whole.

The snake feels for movement in the muscles, lungs, and heart of its prey and responds by tightening its coils.

SPITTING VENOM A few species of African cobra, such as this Mozambique spitting cobra, spit venom into the eyes of predators. Each fang has a small opening, through which venom is forced at high pressure.

The venom can reach 10 feet (3 m) away and can cause irritation or even blindness in its target.

The snake’s strong muscles help it to hang from branches.

REPTILES VENOM AND FANGS Some snakes have a pair of highly modified teeth in the upper jaw called fangs that deliver toxins called venom. In a lightning-quick strike, the fangs stab the victim and inject venom into it. In vipers (right), the venom causes swelling, blood loss, and can even destroy tissue. Other snakes use venom to paralyze their prey.

Snakes are formidable, highly efficient predators. They detect prey by sensing smell, warmth, vibrations caused by movement, and sometimes also by sight. Snakes swallow their prey whole, but must subdue and kill them first. Constrictors squeeze their victims, but other snakes use venom— lethal toxins delivered by needle-sharp fangs—to kill.

HOW SNAKES KILL The fang injects venom the snake’s prey.

The compressor muscle squeezes to force venom out of the venom gland.

This bird gulps for air as it is squeezed.

Heat-sensitive pits detect warm- blooded prey.

The snake’s flexible lower jaw expands to swallow prey.

Venom flows down the venom canal inside the fang.

The venom gland produces venom.

REPTILES The quadrate bone, which connects the top and bottom jaws, allows the jaws to open wider.

▶ EATING AN EGG An egg-eating snake can swallow an egg that is more than two times the diameter of its head and body. The egg is then crushed inside the snake, and its rich, nutritious contents digested.

Throat muscles push the egg down.

Flexible skin between the snake’s scales stretches.

The snake may wedge the egg against firm objects like rocks or stones to help maneuver the egg into its throat.

The lower jaw can separate at the front to allow it to expand sideways.

Although snakes don’t have limbs and can’t chew, they manage to eat by using some clever techniques. Their jaws are flexible enough to engulf food wider than their own bodies. Practically all snakes have sharp teeth to subdue struggling prey, but egg-eating snakes are toothless and have other adaptations for their diet.

HOW SNAKES EAT OPEN WIDE The ligaments of the snake’s jaws open wide enough to take the egg in, as the snake pushes down slightly to get its bottom jaw under the egg.

DOWN THE THROAT Once swallowed, powerful throat muscles push the egg into the snake’s throat. At this stage, the egg is still completely intact.

As the egg begins to break, the snake’s muscles squeeze out its contents.

The eggshell comes out as crushed pellets.

Arching its back, the snake uses its muscles to force out the pellets of crushed shell.

The snake opens its jaws wide to regurgitate the shell.

Downward- pointing spines The shell cracks when the bony spines move back and forth against it.

Muscles CRACKING THE EGG The egg travels down the throat until it reaches a part of the backbone that has downward-pointing spines. The snake arches its back to crack the egg and split its membrane. The egg is crushed and compressed, and the contents are squeezed down into the snake’s stomach.

REGURGITATION Once the food has been extracted, what is left of the shell is regurgitated from the snake’s mouth. The snake may be able to go for a month without eating again.

REPTILES Tortoises and turtles have their own body armor: thick bony shells that cover their bodies, into which they can withdraw their vulnerable head and limbs. Tortoises live on land, and tend to have high-domed shells to protect them from predators. Turtles, which live in water, have smoother, streamlined shells that make swimming easier.

HOW TORTOISES WORK TURNING HEADS When danger threatens, a turtle or a tortoise can pull its head into its shell. Members of one group—called the side-necked turtles—bend their head sideways under the roof of the shell. Others, including all land tortoises, are called hidden-necked.

They withdraw their neck back into the shell so the head stays facing forward.

SHELL CONSTRUCTION The hard plates that make up a tortoise’s or turtle’s outer shell are formed from big, specialized scales called scutes. Each forms a horny outer casing over a bony base under the skin. The upper shell— called the carapace—forms a dome over the back. The flatter lower shell—the plastron—covers the belly.

Horny scutes Bony layer Backbone The bright orange and black pattern of the shell’s underside may help scare off predators.

The big, jagged scutes will get smoother with age.

The ribs are part of the shell.

A side-necked turtle’s neck is powerful enough to be able to flip the turtle upright if it is upside down.

Plastron Carapace SIDE VIEW OF INDIAN ROOFED TURTLE

REPTILES ◀ SAFE FROM HARM With its head and feet safely retracted inside, this baby Indian roofed turtle is well protected. Like many other kinds of turtles, the shell changes shape as it matures, turning into a smoother dome. Indian roofed turtles live in rivers in south Asia.

Folds of skin around the neck encircle the head as it is retracted.

The limbs and feet can close in around the head to help protect the face.

UNDER THE SEA Many turtles have webbed feet for swimming, but only the larger ocean-going turtles have flippers. Marine turtles spend most of their life in water, but like all turtles, they lay hard-shelled eggs, so they must crawl onto a sandy beach to bury them on land.

A high ridge called a keel is colored red in baby Indian roofed turtles.

Birds evolved from dinosaurs more than 150 million years ago, and are close relatives of modern reptiles. All birds are covered in feathers and have wings, which most species use to fly.

This power of flight has allowed birds to live all over the world. Some birds even travel great distances each year to spend the winter months in warmer climates.

BIRDS The defining feature of birds is their feathers, which no other living animals have. Feathers cover birds’ bodies and fulfill many different functions, from enabling flight to providing insulation, camouflage, and bright coloration for displays that help birds find mates. Birds are also the only living vertebrates apart from bats to possess wings. Other key features include beaks, lightweight hollow bones, and massively powerful flight muscles.

HOW BIRDS WORK BREATHING Birds’ lungs are connected to nine air sacs, which keep air flowing through the lungs in one direction. This makes birds much better at extracting oxygen from the air than other animals, giving them enough energy for flight.

Air sac Trachea, or windpipe Lung Birds have large eyes and excellent color vision for spotting predators or food.

The crown feathers can be raised into a crest when the bird is trying to attract a mate.

This northern cardinal has a short, cone-shaped beak for crunching seeds.

Air with less oxygen flows through the upper air sacs and out of the windpipe.

Oxygen-rich air flows through the lower air sacs and into the lungs.

A bird’s streamlined body shape makes flight possible.

To save weight and make flight easier, the beak has no teeth.

However, it is made of a hard material called keratin and is strong enough to crush food.

The breast contains enormous flight muscles.

BIRDS TYPES OF BIRD There are more than 10,000 species of bird in the world. These are divided into 36 major groups called orders, which are divided in turn into 236 families.

BIRDS OF PREY These predators and scavengers have hooked beaks and talons for hunting, and very sharp eyesight for spotting prey. They include falcons, hawks, eagles, ospreys, and vultures.

Streamlined flight feathers allow air to flow smoothly over the wing.

Long tail feathers help birds balance when perching and steer when flying.

Long, clawed toes give grip for perching.

Bands of tissue in a perching bird’s leg make its toes automatically lock around a perch when it lands on one. This means the bird can stay perching even when asleep.

WATERFOWL Ducks, swans, and geese have webbed feet for swimming, and most have broad, flattened beaks to help them forage for food. They spend much of their lives on or near water.

FLIGHTLESS BIRDS Five families of birds are known as the flightless birds, or ratites. They have powerful legs and feet for running.

◀ PERCHING BIRD This northern cardinal belongs to the largest group of birds, called the perching birds, or passerines.

All perching bird species have a long, strong hind toe adapted for gripping branches or other perches. This extremely varied group includes over half of all birds—around 5,800 species.

This male bird’s colorful feathers help him attract a mate. In many bird species, males have more colorful feathers than females.

BIRDS Birds have strong, streamlined bodies and highly specialized skeletons for efficient, aerodynamic flight. Their bones are hollow to save weight, but reinforced with internal struts for strength. Their breastbones anchor powerful wing muscles, and their compact body shape helps them perch upright on two legs.

HOW BIRD SKELETONS WORK ▶ FALCON SKELETON The peregrine falcon makes high-speed dives at prey and kills it on impact. To withstand this, it has a streamlined, rigid skeleton. Like all strong flying birds, the falcon has an enlarged keel, which allows for larger flight muscles and greater flapping power.

The keel supports the falcon’s powerful flight muscles.

The neck has many bones called vertebrae, which make it highly flexible.

Eye orbit (opening)

for large eyes Overlapping projections in the rib cage give the skeleton extra rigidity.

A strong bone called the coracoid holds each shoulder away from the body and helps lift the shoulders during flight.

In some birds, the upper part of the beak can move independently of the rest of the skull. Birds have no teeth, possibly to save weight.

The wishbone is springy enough to store energy with each wingbeat during flight.

There is a space between the bird’s lower wing bones (the ulna and the radius) for the muscles that flex the end of the wing.

The hand bones are fused to withstand the impact of flight.

A ring of bone called the sclerotic ring holds the falcon’s tubular eyeball in place. This means that it cannot swivel its eyes, but it has a highly flexible neck so can compensate by turning its head.

BIRD Birds have between 10 and 25 neck bones, which gives them great flexibility.

HUMAN Like all mammals, humans have only seven neck bones, which gives us limited flexibility.

GIRAFFE Even giraffes only have seven neck bones.

However, each one is around 10 in (25 cm) long.

NECK BONES Birds have long and highly flexible neck,s with more neck bones than most other animals. This allows them to preen themselves and twist around to get a better view of their surroundings.

Most birds have four toes (digits) on their feet.

Short thigh bones and longer leg bones help the bird to balance when it walks.

Sharp claws help the falcon seize its prey.

Fused lower leg bones form the tarsometatarsus.

This joint looks like a knee, but is actually the bird’s ankle.

BREASTBONE A bird’s keel is a large extension of the breastbone to which its huge wing muscles are anchored. Some flightless birds do not have a keel.

Strong leg bones support the bird’s body weight.

One toe faces backward to aid perching.

A bird’s tail feathers are attached to a short stump of fused bones.

Keel This muscle lowers the wing.

This muscle raises the wing.

FISH EATERS Herons and many other fish eaters have long, daggerlike beaks for grabbing prey. They rarely stab their food. Instead, their beaks have serrated edges to grip slippery fishes.

MEAT EATERS Birds of prey, such as this golden eagle, use their sharply hooked beaks to tear meat. Most kill with their powerful talons, but some, such as falcons, use their beaks to inflict a fatal bite.

A patch of leathery skin called the cere surrounds the nostrils.

Upper and lower jaw bones make up the beak.

The beak is covered in a tough layer of skin.

The sharp hook at the tip of an eagle’s beak can rip off fur and feathers, and tear flesh.

A dagger-shaped beak can spear a fish, but birds rarely do this.

This egret’s long, flexible neck allows it to strike quickly.

GOLDEN EAGLE GREAT EGRET

BIRDS More than any other part of its body, a bird’s beak, or bill, gives the best clue about what it eats. Over millions of years, beaks have evolved into a range of shapes and sizes.

Predators have sharp beaks that work like weapons for hunting and fishing, while plant eaters need grasping beaks to pull at fruit, or strong ones to crush nuts and seeds.

TYPES OF BEAK NECTAR FEEDERS Hummingbirds have long, pointed beaks to probe flowers for nectar, which they lap up with their long tongue. The sword-billed hummingbird’s beak is especially long to reach into tubular flowers.

SEED EATERS Birds such as finches have stout, cone-shaped beaks that can deliver crushing force to split seeds open. There are countless variations on this basic shape according to the type of seed that a bird eats. Seeds are wedged in a special groove in the upper beak for cracking.

FILTER FEEDERS Flamingos have uniquely shaped beaks that are lined with fringes like the bristles on a broom. A flamingo lowers its slightly open beak upside down into water and pumps with its tongue so the bristles trap particles of food.

FRUIT AND NUT EATERS A parrot’s beak has a flexible hinge in both its upper and lower halves.

This means the parrot can use it to manipulate food and grasp and split fruit and nuts. The beak is so flexible that a parrot can also use it as a “third foot” for climbing.

A sword-billed hummingbird’s beak is longer than the rest of its body.

The thick, sturdy beak crushes a seed’s tough casing.

A sharp hook is ideal for grasping fruit.

The curved shape of a flamingo’s beak helps it collect food.

LESSER FLAMINGO BLACK-HEADED PARROT GOULDIAN FINCH SWORD-BILLED HUMMINGBIRD

BIRDS Birds are excellent fliers, with massive flight muscles that make up at least a quarter of their entire body weight. Their feathers provide a streamlined body covering and allow the position and shape of their wings and tail to be changed for precise control while flying. Their skeletons are extremely light so as not to impede flight, but strong enough to withstand the impact of flight.

▼ SOARING FLIGHT To save energy and gain height for spotting prey, birds of prey such as this golden eagle favor soaring and gliding over flapping flight. With long wings that span 7.5 feet (2.3 m), the golden eagle is well suited to this gliding style of flight.

HOW BIRDS FLY The tail feathers are fanned out to create a force called lift that keeps the bird in the air.

The outer tips of the main flight feathers are spread out for slow, gliding flight.

A group of feathers called the alula is held open in slow flight to stop the eagle stalling.

FLAPPING WINGS Birds flap their wings to generate the two forces necessary for flight: thrust and lift.

By flapping their wings, birds push themselves forward and produce the thrust that counteracts drag, or air resistance. Once the bird is airborne, lift is created by the stream of air that pushes over the curved surfaces of the bird’s outstretched wings.

Downstroke Folded wings DOWNSTROKE As this eagle lowers its wings in what is called a downstroke, the air pressure underneath the wings pushes it forward and upward.

UPSTROKE The eagle begins to raise its wings in an upstroke. It partially folds its wings to save energy and reduce forces that would drag it downward.

These feathers are called coverts and create a smooth surface for air to flow over.

Flight is powered by enormous breast muscles.

The eagle’s streamlined body shape aids flight.

These feathers remain close together to divert air over the wing.

The lightweight beak contains no teeth.

These feathers are called marginal coverts and protect the eagle’s wing bones.

Gaps in the wing reduce air resistance.

Powerful talons can kill prey on impact when the eagle swoops down on it.

Unfolded wings The legs are lowered to prepare for landing.

RAISED WINGS The eagle unfolds its wings once they are fully raised, ready for another downstroke or soaring flight.

COMING IN TO LAND The eagle holds its wings in a shallow V for slow gliding flight, and starts to lower its legs as it prepares to land.

AIR ON THE WING As an eagle glides, the angle and shape of its wing diverts the flowing air. Airflow over the wing is faster and the pressure is lowered; under the wing, it is slower and the pressure is raised.

This pressure difference creates an upward force called lift.

The angle of the wing makes the air flow faster over it, so the downward pressure on the wing is lower.

Cross-section of the bird’s wing Air under the wing flows more slowly, so the upward pressure on the wing is higher.

The higher pressure below the wing creates lift.

BIRDS Birds’ wings are light, strong, and flexible, and enable them to fly faster and farther than any other animal. With a front edge that is thicker than the back, and feathers that narrow to a point and create a streamlined surface for air to flow over, wings are ideally shaped to keep birds in the air. The wings are anchored to the body by light, hollow bones and controlled by powerful chest muscles.

Tertiary flight feathers cover the space between the body and the wing.

A group of feathers called the alula acts like a flap to control the bird’s lift at slow speeds.

HOW WINGS WORK

BIRDS WING SHAPES Although they are all constructed in the same basic way, bird wings have developed into a variety of different shapes and sizes to allow them to flap, glide, hover, and swoop through the sky.

The shape of a bird’s wings is directly related to its flight needs.

For example, some birds have short wings for maneuverability, while others have long wings for soaring for hours.

GLIDING Most gliding birds’ wings are longer than they are wide. Instead of being continually flapped, these wings are often held out to catch the wind.

HIGH-SPEED WINGS Fast-flying birds have slender wings that end in a narrow point.

These wings can be flapped in rapid bursts to reach high speeds quickly.

SOARING Many birds of prey have long, broad wings that help them soar on rising currents of warm air.

Slotted feathers at the wing tips help keep the bird in the air.

MANEUVERING Many forest birds have short, rounded wings that enable tight maneuvering in confined spaces and fast takeoffs to evade predators.

WING BONES Apart from the shoulder, bird wings have two main joints that are equivalent to a human elbow and wrist. These are the joints that make the wing open, shut, and swivel, which are the crucial elements for flight.

◀ FALCON WING This peregrine falcon’s pointed wings help it make high-speed dives to kill its prey on impact. The peregrine falcon is a swift, agile predator that can reach diving speeds of 200 mph (320 kph), and is probably the fastest bird in the sky.

Under-wing coverts overlap the bases of the other feathers.

Ten long, strong, and stiff primary flight feathers The leading edge is the first part of the wing that connects with the air.

This bone anchors the wing to the body.

The primary flight feathers are attached to these fused bones.

The secondary flight feathers are attached to this bone.

Primary fl ight feathers pro pel the bird through the air .

FALCON COOPER’S HAWK ALBATROSS GOLDEN EAGLE

BIRDS Feathers are extremely tough, yet light and flexible, and make up just 5–10 percent of a bird’s weight. They grow from the skin and are made of keratin, like human fingernails and hair. Each feather is anchored in a small pit called a follicle and grows within a tube called a feather sheath. Once a feather is fully grown, it unfolds and the feather sheath falls away.

HOW FEATHERS WORK STREAMLINED SURFACE The central shaft of a feather has many side branches (barbs), all of which in turn have many smaller branches (barbules). Some barbules have tiny hooks (barbicels) that lock onto the barbules underneath them, creating a sleek surface for flight.

PHEASANT PLUMAGE Like that of most birds, the ring-necked pheasant’s plumage consists of several different kinds of feathers. Contour feathers cover all of its body apart from the beak, legs, and bare patches on the face. Down feathers under the contour feathers provide insulation.

The lower section of the feather shaft is called the quill. It sprouts from a follicle in the skin. Unlike the rest of the shaft, it is hollow.

The barbicels are hooks that lock the barbules together.

The exposed parts of feathers can be colorful, but hidden parts are usually dull.

The solid part of the feather shaft is called the rachis. It provides support for the barbs.

Barbules Tail feathers Contour feathers Down feathers under the contour feathers Primary flight feathers Secondary flight feathers Parallel barbs The downy base of this tail feather insulates the bird’s skin.

BIRDS ▼ ANATOMY OF A FEATHER A bird’s central tail feathers are often its longest. Like all tail feathers, this pheasant feather has a curved, aerodynamic shape and a smooth, streamlined surface formed of parallel barbs and interlocking barbules. This surface allows a set of tail feathers to provide balance and steering in flight.

The tip is the first part of a growing feather to emerge.

The wider side of a feather is called the inner vane. It often has a different pattern from the narrow side (the outer vane).

Parallel barbs and interlocking barbules create a smooth surface.

Exposed, overlapping section of a contour feather Secondary flight feathers give a wing its curved shape.

The wide inner vane faces away from the wind when the bird is in flight.

Freely moving barbs TYPES OF FEATHER Feathers come in two main groups.

Those with a thick, firm rachis (central shaft), which serves as a strengthening rod, include the contour, flight, and tail feathers. Those with a thinner rachis are soft, delicate, and fluffy, and include the down feathers.

PRIMARY FLIGHT FEATHER The outermost part of the wing, known as the “hand,” is made up of 9–12 primary flight feathers, which propel a bird through the air.

SECONDARY FLIGHT FEATHER Flight feathers are longer and stiffer than the rest of the plumage.

Secondary flight feathers are attached to a bird’s upper arm bone and sustain the bird in the air.

CONTOUR FEATHER Contour feathers tightly follow the shape of a bird’s body. They are densely packed together and overlap like roof tiles to give a streamlined surface.

DOWN FEATHER Down feathers do not have interlocking hooks (barbicels), so their barbs can move freely.

They trap pockets of air to keep a bird warm.

The narrow side of a feather is called the outer vane.

A primary flight feather’s narrow outer vane helps it slice through the air.

Downy section for insulation

BIRDS TIRELESS WINGS Hummingbird wings typically beat about 80 times per second, but during the excitement of its courtship flight, this rufous hummingbird can exceed 200 beats per second. Their small but powerful wings allow some hummingbirds to reach speeds of 62 mph (100 kph).

HOW HUMMINGBIRDS HOVER Strong muscles, a rapid wing beat, and flexible joints are the keys to a hummingbird’s flight technique.

Unlike other birds, which mainly use the downstroke of their wings to keep them in the air, hummingbirds have a powerful upstroke, too.

The wings move too fast for the human eye to track, making them appear as a blur.

The feathers have a metallic sheen and appear to change color when the bird moves.

The beating wings produce a deep humming sound, which gives the bird its name.

A hummingbird’s wings move horizontally backward and forward, which helps it to stay steady in the air.

The wings twist back around and down.

DOWNSTROKE The downstroke provides lift, which keeps the bird in the air, and thrust, which causes it to go forward.

On this stroke, the wings are brought rapidly forward and down.

FIGURE EIGHT At the end of the downstroke, flexible shoulder joints allow the wings to trace a smooth figure-eight shape, which minimizes loss of energy as the wings change direction.

UPSTROKE As the wings sweep back, the upstroke provides an equal thrust to that of the downstroke. These forces cancel each other out, which means the bird stays in a fixed position in the air.

No birds live quite as frantically as hummingbirds.

They are so skilled in flight that they can hover and even fly backward, their wings beating with such speed that they appear as a blur. Such a busy body requires a heart that beats more than ten times faster than ours, and a diet that consists almost entirely of energy-rich nectar.

HOW HUMMINGBIRDS HOVER

BIRDS ▼ FUELLING STATION The sugar in nectar supplies hummingbirds with energy.

They need to hover to keep their position in front of flowers while they lap up the sweet liquid with their long flicking tongue. Like other kinds of hummingbird, this chestnut-breasted coronet gets 90 percent of its food from nectar. The rest of its diet consists of small insects, which provide the protein it needs for growth.

Hummingbirds’ bills vary in length to feed from different kinds of flower.

Large breast muscles make up 30 percent of the bird’s body weight and help power the wings.

Highly mobile shoulder joints allow the wing to rotate 180 degrees, as they pivot back and forth.

The tail feathers hang vertically, helping the bird to balance during flight.

The tiny, light feet are useful for perching only.

The pollen of the rainbow heliconia flower sticks to the hummingbird and may pollinate the next flower the bird visits.

The head stays still while the bird hovers and feeds.

A dense flock of wild budgerigars descends from the Australian sky. These small, seed-eating parrots often fly large distances in search of food and water. When they breed, their numbers can grow rapidly, creating huge flocks of several thousand birds. Flying in large flocks has many benefits—it helps birds save energy, and gives them a much better chance of spotting or deterring predators.

LIFE ON THE MOVE

BIRDS FINDING THE WAY Migrating birds can sense the approximate time of day. By comparing this with the angle of the Sun’s rays, they know which way is north, south, east, and west. Other navigational aids include the stars, landmarks such as mountains and coasts, smells, and the Earth’s magnetic field, which birds may be able to detect using their eyes.

In birds, migration involves flight from one region to another as the seasons change. This means they can breed in one place and spend the winter elsewhere, to find food and escape harsh weather.

Changes in day length and temperature alert birds that it is time to move on. Migration takes a great deal of energy, so birds eat a lot before taking off and along the way. Many also fly in large flocks for safety, or in a V-shape to save energy.

HOW BIRDS MIGRATE Birds see the setting sun in the evening and know that it is in the west.

Huge, broad wings allow whooper swans to glide on the wind.

Birds in the southern hemisphere see the sun at its highest point and know that it is north of them.

The sun is at its highest point in the middle of the day.

Birds in the northern hemisphere see the sun at its highest point and know that it is south of them.

This young whooper swan is strong enough to make its first migration journey.

Birds see the rising sun in the morning and know that it is in the east.

BIRDS ▼ EFFICIENT FLIGHT Whooper swans breed in Scandinavia and Siberia and migrate southward to countries including the UK and Japan. Like many birds, they migrate in flocks. Before taking off, they feed intensively to lay down fat to fuel the journey. Once airborne, they can maintain a steady 47 mph (75 kph). They can fly over 185 miles (300 km) in a single day, but may stop en route to rest and refuel. They may also change course to avoid bad weather.

POLE TO POLE Arctic terns breed in the Arctic and migrate to spend October to March in Antarctica, avoiding the Arctic winter. Their round trip is nearly 60,000 miles (100,000 km) long. This means some terns fly the distance of three return trips to the moon in their lifetime.

V-FORMATION Migrating geese fly in a V-shape and take turns to lead the flock. Apart from the leader, each bird flies in air currents created by the bird in front, saving energy.

The leader sets the pace.

Following birds save energy.

The streamlined neck and body shape reduce air resistance to make flight easier.

Long, flexible flight feathers at the tip of the wing help to reduce drag, making flight more efficient.

Fat reserves power huge breast muscles for several hours of non-stop flight.

LONG VOYAGES Bar-tailed godwits migrate from Alaska to New Zealand in a fast, non-stop flight of eight days—a record among birds. Before setting off, their bodies turn non-essential tissue into fat and muscle.

Non-stop route over Pacific Ocean The birds fly south along the coast of Africa or South America.

Alaska New Zealand The return journey includes a stopover in China, so the birds are in good condition for breeding upon their arrival in Alaska.

BIRDS ▶ DAZZLED WITH COLOR A peacock fans his colorful feathers to impress a female. The spots of blue and gold, which look like eyes, are thought to improve his chances—but he must parade them at just the right angle.

The female peafowl—called a peahen— is somber in comparison, but her drab feathers help her to avoid attracting predators when she raises her family alone.

MALE INDIAN PEAFOWL (PEACOCK)

COURTSHIP IN OTHER BIRDS In some birds, males display in groups called leks, so a female can compare and choose.

These males mate with many females and play no part in raising offspring. But many birds form more stable partnerships and even share parental duties.

GREATER BIRD OF PARADISE With heads bowed and upended tail plumes, two male greater birds of paradise combine color with acrobatics as they display to watching females at the top of rainforest trees.

BOWERBIRD Male bowerbirds build structures out of sticks known as bowers, and then add blue decorations, such as flowers. The green-colored female judges the results and chooses the best architect.

RED-CROWNED CRANE The courtship ritual of the red-crowned crane involves both sexes. Their elegant leaping dance and loud honking duet seal a bond that pairs them in parenthood, and for life.

The peacock’s train grows from the base of his back, just above his tail.

The colors are iridescent, which means they change hue as they move in the light.

In the contest to get a mate, some male birds use song, dance, and vivid colors to grab the attention of females. This behavior is called courtship. Because females tend to prefer males with the biggest and brightest feathers, many male birds have evolved spectacular plumage and extravagant rituals.

HOW COURTSHIP WORKS

BIRDS The length of the male’s train feathers increases with age.

Muscles at the base of each feather contract to raise the train.

Both male and female have a head crest.

FEMALE PEAFOWL (PEAHEN)

The male gets his eyespots in his third year.

Each of the 100–150 feathers can reach up to

## 6.5 feet (2 m) in length

BIRDS A bird’s nest provides a safe place to incubate eggs and raise chicks, keeping them warm and hidden from predators. Most birds build one at the start of the breeding season and abandon it when the young are old enough to fly, but many storks, herons, and birds of prey use theirs year after year. Birds’ nests come in all shapes and sizes—the simplest are just a cliff ledge, hole in a tree, or hollow in the ground.

▼ HANGING OUT Many species in the weaver family, such as this southern masked weaver from Africa, construct intricate nests suspended from a branch. Only the male builds the main structure; the female helps to line it with feathers for warmth. For added security, the birds often breed in small colonies and choose spots over a river or water hole. If a weaver does build a nest alone, he may build it near a wasp nest or beneath an eagle nest for extra protection.

HOW NESTS WORK BUILDING A NEST A male southern masked weaver often builds several nests at a time in an attempt to find a mate.

If the nest doesn’t win a female bird’s approval, the male has to dismantle it and start again.

WEAVING A SPIRAL The male winds strands of grass around the branch that will support the nest.

MAKING STIRRUPS More grass is added to make stirrups that support the weight of the bird.

FORMING A RING The two stirrups are joined to form a ring, which is thickened to make the scaffold of the nest.

CREATING A CHAMBER The ring is extended to form a chamber with an entrance at the bottom. The male and female then line the inside of the nest.

The nest hangs from the end of a thin, sagging branch, making it harder for predators to reach.

The nest is neatly woven from fresh grass, occasionally with a few extra leaves.

BIRDS TYPES OF NEST In general, nests reflect a bird’s size and habitat.

A wealth of materials may be used, from sticks and twigs to leaves, moss, fur, mud, pebbles, and pieces of plastic or other garbage.

CUP NESTS A cup is one of the most common nest shapes, especially among small birds—and hummingbirds build some of the smallest of all.

They bind the minute structure together with spiders' webs.

CAVITY NESTS Tree hollows are occupied by a wide range of birds, from woodpeckers and owls to tits and parrots. Wood shavings and feathers are often the only lining.

ADHERENT NESTS Swifts fasten their nests to cliffs, caves, and walls, using mud or plant material mixed with saliva.

This edible swiftlet nest is made entirely from saliva.

PLATFORM NESTS The bald eagle, like many big birds of prey, builds a huge treetop stick nest. Pairs mate for life, and their nest grows every year as the male and female add new material.

FLOATING NESTS Some waterbirds, such as this great crested grebe, construct raftlike nests from aquatic vegetation. The nests are safe from predators on land, and rise and fall with the water level.

The weaver’s sharp, conical beak is ideal for cutting strips of grass.

Strong feet with long claws make hanging upside down easier.

BIRDS Unlike baby mammals, which develop inside the mother’s body, baby birds develop inside eggs. An egg is packed with the nutrients that the embryo—the unhatched bird—needs to grow. The time from laying to hatching varies between species, from just ten days for woodpeckers to 80 days for large albatrosses.

HOW EGGS DEVELOP GROWING AND HATCHING A duck embryo takes four weeks to develop fully. By this time, it is squashed inside the egg with its head tucked in over its belly.

Once the duckling is ready to hatch, it breaks through the shell of the egg with its beak, cuts a circle around the shell, and pushes the end away to emerge into the outside world.

GROWING EMBRYO At first, the egg is one huge cell. While the parent bird sits on it to keep it warm, the cell begins to divide many times, forming the different organs and tissues. This is sustained by the nutrients and water stored in the egg. Shining a light through the egg allows us to see what is happening inside.

BREAKING THE SHELL The chick is ready to hatch after 28 days. It breaks the air sac inside the egg and breathes oxygen for the first time. It then repeatedly strikes the shell with its egg tooth—a sharp projection on its beak.

Eventually, it breaks through the shell and widens the hole to allow more air in.

The yolk and other ﬂ uids in the egg y y nourish the embryo.

The chick wriggles in the shell, which weakens it.

The chick cuts a circle around the shell.

The embryo’s eyes are developing.

y y The chick gradually chips away at the shell to widen the hole.

y y DAY 1: FRESHLY LAID EGG WEEK 1 WEEK 2 DAY 28 DAY 28 DAY 28 The shell has tiny holes that allow air to pass through it.

An air sac at the blunt end of the egg will provide oxygen once the chick can breathe on its own.

Pushing with its feet, the chick emerges rear end ﬁ rst.

g d ﬁ g As the embryo grows, the yolk is used up.

g g Heart INSIDE AN EGG The shell and protective membranes enclose the nourishing yolk, albumen (egg white) that cushions the embryo, and an air sac. Ropelike strands called chalazae secure the yolk.

Chalazae Developing embryo Albumen, or white The yolk is rich in fat and protein.

y y Egg tooth on beak Air sac Shell Membranes

BIRDS BLOOD VESSELS A network of blood vessels, seen here inside an empty egg after hatching, supplies the embryo with oxygen and takes away waste carbon dioxide.

HATCHING The chick extends the hole to cut a circle around the egg and pushes the blunt end of the shell away with its feet. During the final push, it rotates its body and wriggles out of the membranes that have surrounded it.

The small, stumpy wings are weak and lack flight feathers.

The feathers are wet and untidy at first, but will dry out and fluff up within a few hours of hatching.

The duckling must also break through the shell membranes that protected it from bacteria.

The duckling hatches fully alert, with its eyes open.

A fully developed chick has a muscle along the back of its head and neck to help it strike and break the shell as it hatches.

BIRDS GROWING UP As its skeleton grows, the once- plump duckling develops a longer neck and the beginnings of a tail. Its beak and wing buds grow and the webbing on its feet expands. But the duckling is still fluffy—it lacks any adult feathers.

EARLY STAGES Although the duckling needs a rest after breaking out of its egg, it already has open eyes and a full covering of warm down. Soon, it is able to run, swim, and eat, though for safety it stays near its mother.

The legs and feet are too weak to support the chick.

The wing buds remain small but are growing fast.

Growing wing bud The feathers are still damp from being in the egg.

The beak has hardened and started to lengthen.

NEWLY HATCHED ONE DAY OLD 18 DAYS OLD FOUR WEEKS OLD EGG TOOTH A chick’s beak is relatively soft—too soft to break through an eggshell. However, chicks have a small, hard projection called an egg tooth on their beaks to help them hatch. Newly hatched chicks still have their egg tooth, but it falls off 2–4 days after hatching.

The downy feathers have dried out.

The duckling can now walk upright.

The tail is just visible.

Longer neck The beak has developed fine, toothlike projections along its edges.

Fluffy down still covers the body.

Wing bud

BIRDS FULL SIZE By now, the duck looks just like its parents.

Its plumage is made up of several different types of feathers, and its enormous breast muscles are fully formed, enabling the young adult duck to fly for the first time.

The development of baby birds varies enormously.

Some, such as waterfowl, gamebirds, and shorebirds, can walk within hours of hatching and soon learn to fend for themselves. In contrast, most songbirds start life weak, naked, and blind, so they depend completely on their parents for food, warmth, and shelter. Some large seabirds and birds of prey take months to become independent.

HOW BIRDS GROW ▼ DEVELOPING DUCK Waterfowl such as this Pekin duck are known as precocial birds, which means their young are relatively independent upon hatching and can soon walk and swim. After just 16 weeks, the duck has developed all the features that make it so well suited to living on or near water, such as waterproofed feathers and fully expanded webbing on its feet.

The duck’s wings are fully grown, so it is able to fly.

Growing flight feathers SIX  WEEKS OLD 16 WEEKS OLD FLIGHT FEATHERS This duck’s flight feathers can be seen growing inside tubes called feather sheaths. The feathers start growing as pulp inside the sheaths, and the tips gradually emerge to form blades. Once a feather is fully grown, the sheath falls away.

Adult feathers replace the fluffy down.

Complete set of white adult feathers The beak and legs have the orange color of an adult duck.

BIRDS ▶ IMPOSTOR By the time they are ready to fly, young cuckoos are nearly always far bigger than the adult birds that look after them. Here, a reed warbler struggles to feed an insect to a young Eurasian cuckoo, dwarfed by the outsized imposter it has raised.

The adult reed warbler must work hard to satisfy the appetite of the huge cuckoo chick.

The orange mouth lining may warn predators not to approach the nest.

Most birds raise their own young, but some get others to do the job for them. Female cuckoos lay their eggs in the nests of small songbirds, one in each nest. When a baby cuckoo hatches, it gets rid of any other eggs or young in the nest. Despite this, its foster parents never realize that the bird is an impostor, and instinctively keep feeding it until it is old enough to fly away.

HOW CUCKOOS WORK

BIRDS TAKEOVER BID An adult male cuckoo will sometimes lure a host bird away from its nest so that the female cuckoo can slip in. She pushes one of the eggs out of the nest, and then lays her own egg.

The cuckoo egg resembles the eggs already in the nest. When the young cuckoo hatches, it ejects all the other eggs—and even any hatched chicks.

MOUTH MIMICS Unlike cuckoos, young African whydahs do not destroy the other eggs in the nest.

They let them hatch, but claim a share of food by imitating their nest-mates, gaping for food to reveal similar markings inside the mouth.

This cuckoo’s wing feathers are fully grown, so it will soon be able to fly away.

When fully developed, the cuckoo leaves the nest, but still may be fed by its foster parents.

The pin-tailed whydah chick’s mouth mimics that of a common waxbill chick.

Common waxbill chick EGG DECEPTION Cuckoos lay eggs with patterns that match the eggs of their victims. Each female targets a species that offers the best match, although the cuckoo egg is usually bigger.

FIRST OUT The cuckoo egg develops faster than the other eggs and usually hatches before them. The young cuckoo is naked and blind.

KILLER INSTINCT Within hours, the young cuckoo works each of the other eggs (or hatched chicks) onto its back and pushes them out of the nest. It does this to stop other chicks competing with it for food.

BIRDS HUNTING BY EAR The great grey owl’s right ear opens slightly lower than its left ear, which means sound from below arrives at the right ear fractionally sooner than it does at the left ear.

The owl can use this time difference to work out the exact location of its prey, even when the prey is hidden under leaves or snow.

Prey’s sound waves Owls are stealth hunters that use their keen senses, sharp claws, and soft feathers to swoop silently from their perch and snatch unsuspecting prey. Armed with super-sensitive hearing and vision, some owls can pinpoint the exact location of a moth in the dark or a mouse scampering under snow or fallen leaves. Most owls are nocturnal but some species, such as the great grey owl, also hunt by day.

A great grey owl does not have ear tufts, unlike many owls.

▶ FACIAL DISC The great grey owl’s most striking feature is its large facial disc made from a concave ring of feathers. Each side of the face acts as one half of the disc, focusing sound toward each ear. The owl can adjust its facial feathers to focus sound even more precisely. The disc is especially big in this species to help it detect prey under snow.

HOW OWLS SENSE PREY The facial disc collects sound energy and funnels it towards the ears.

The left ear opens slightly higher than the right ear, so sound reaches it later.

BIRDS HEAD ROTATION Owls have large, tubular-shaped eyes that cannot move in their sockets. However, owls can rotate their heads 270 degrees in each direction, allowing them to look behind themselves without moving their bodies.

SILENT FLIGHT Some species of owl, including the great grey owl and the tawny owl, have a serrated, comb- like edge on their outer primary flight feathers.

This muffles the sound of the owl’s beating wings and enables its flight to be almost silent, so it can swoop down on its prey without being heard.

The beak is short enough not to interfere with the owl’s vision.

This tawny owl feather has a serrated edge.

The feathers form a disc shape, which directs sound toward the owl’s ears.

Its forward-facing eyes give 3D vision, helping the owl to judge distance.

0° 90° 270° 180°

SOARING ON THERMALS Eagles save energy by soaring on thermals—rising columns of warm air. They circle upward before gliding to the next thermal and soaring again. This means they can keep at great heights to scan for prey without flapping their wings.

HUNTING TECHNIQUE Bald eagles swoop down onto water, drop their feet below the surface to catch a fish, and fly off with it. They sometimes steal fishes from other birds, such as ospreys, and also scavenge on dead fishes left by bears or leftovers from human picnics.

HIGH-RESOLUTION VISION Compared with humans, eagles have five times as many light receptors in the part of the eye that resolves images most sharply. They can see prey clearly at distances of 1.9 miles (3 km).

EAGLE VISION Each foot has four large claws called talons.

Human-eye, long- distance view Circling The feathers fan out for landing.

Lightweight, overlapping feathers

With their enormous wingspans, hooked beaks, and keen eyesight, eagles are formidable hunters.

They belong to a group known as raptors (birds of prey) and kill with their lethal talons. The largest of the raptors, eagles use their talons to grab animals from the ground or water. They then fly up to a perch and tear their catch apart, using a sharp hook at the tip of the powerful beak to butcher the carcass.

HOW EAGLES HUNT ▲ POWERFUL TALONS The force of this bald eagle’s talons is usually strong enough to kill a fish outright when the eagle seizes it from the water. However, the eagle also has ridges on its feet that help it grip a slippery, struggling fish. The powerful talons can carry prey weighing up to a third of the eagle’s weight.

The broad wings can catch rising air currents, helping the eagle soar.

Ridges on the eagle’s feet help it to grip prey.

BIRDS Birds’ feet come in a variety of sizes and shapes to suit their needs and habitats. Many birds use their feet to hold food and lift it to their mouths. Others use them to perch on twigs and branches. Some birds have feet with stiff spines to hold wriggling fish; others use sharp talons to seize and kill their prey.

TYPES OF BIRD FEET SWIMMING Webbed feet allow birds such as ducks to swim more efficiently, as they can spread out the web and push more water back.

The webbing consists of a continuous flap of skin that joins all of a bird’s three, or sometimes four, toes.

HUNTING Birds of prey, such as ospreys, have powerful talons (claws) for catching and killing prey. An osprey’s strong, hook-shaped talons are used to snatch fish from the water and carry them through the air.

The webbed foot acts as a paddle in the water.

Curved claws enable an osprey to seize fish from the water.

The strong claws can carry heavy loads for long distances.

Spiny pads on an osprey’s toes help it grip struggling prey.

The outer toe can face forward or backward, giving an osprey a tight grip on slippery fish.

OSPREY MALLARD

WADING Birds that wade in shallow water to feed have long toes, often with webbing between them. This spreads their weight to stop them sinking into sand or mud, and helps them maintain their balance.

Their long legs enable them to walk through water without getting their feathers wet.

GRIPPING Owls have very powerful feet, with curved, razor-sharp talons for perching or grasping prey. On each foot, two toes face forward and two backward.

This allows the toes to splay out as far as possible, making the grip even stronger.

RUNNING Flightless birds, such as cassowaries, have muscular legs and sturdy feet.

A cassowary can run at up to 31 mph (50 kmph) and jump up to 4.9 feet (1.5 meters) high. They have a daggerlike claw that they use when they kick animals in defense.

DIVING Many diving birds have webbed feet or stiff, scaly flaps on their toes to propel them in water. The king penguin can reach depths of 1,000 feet (300 meters) and has specially adapted feet to withstand freezing temperatures on the ice.

The talons have sharp tips for piercing flesh.

Powerful, muscular legs allow a cassowary to run at high speed.

Flat, webbed feet help propel a penguin as it dives.

The foot has a long, daggerlike claw.

CASSOWARY KING PENGUIN OWL The front three toes are spread apart, with webbing between them.

For a flamingo, standing on one leg saves energy because it requires less muscle power than standing on two legs.

CHILEAN FLAMINGO

BIRDS ▶ STRONG SWIMMER Ducks such as this mallard swim by pushing and pulling their webbed feet through the water, which propels the duck forward.

Ducks can swim more efficiently by spreading out the webbing of a foot as it pushes back against the water and folding up the web when pulling a foot forward.
